Shepherd on the PS Vita

Shepherd runs a Unity game’s own code on a PS Vita, through a from-scratch stand-in for the Unity engine; its first game is Cult of the Lamb. Work ships in numbered “kits”: each one is built, then run on a real PS Vita and measured. Below is every trick and item in every kit, the ones done and the ones planned, each with a plain explanation and its expected effect. Numbers from kits that ran are measured on the hardware; everything else is an estimate and says so. 650 items in all. By NOVA.

Done · measured on a real VitaPlanned · not built yet

Main Menu on a real PS Vita

with the performance overlay in the top-left corner

On a real PS Vita: only captures from the actual hardware go here. No gameplay yet: the Lamb doesn't walk on the Vita until the memory work (kits 12–13) fits the first room.

Idle Main Menu, frames per second

Best idle-menu app at 444 MHz per kit (red), Play MT at ~500 MHz (gold). Kit 10 was a loading kit (boot −17 to −24 %), so the frame rate stayed about the same.

How to read the effect numbers

Each item shows what it should cut: ms per frame (fewer ms = smoother), fps worked out from that at the menu’s 39.6 ms frame (kit 9, measured), seconds off boot or loading, and MB of memory or card space saved. The label says where the number comes from:

Measured on a real VitaEmulator / MacRoadmap estimateRough guessNo speed effect

The numbers don’t simply add up: many tricks overlap, and a frame only gets faster from the part that was actually slowing it down. Gameplay frames are heavier than the menu, so the same ms there is worth fewer fps (those items show no fps).

Words on this page

Frame, ms, fps
The game draws a new picture (a frame) many times a second. ms is how long one frame takes; fps is frames per second. Fewer ms = more fps = smoother.
CPU, core
The Vita’s main processor. It has 4 cores (workers); games normally get 3, and a plugin unlocks the 4th.
GPU
The graphics chip. It draws the picture while the CPU runs the game.
Memory (RAM), MB / MiB
Where the game keeps everything it’s using right now. The Vita has 512 MB; the game’s share is much smaller, and running out is what stops the Lamb walking today.
Video memory (VRAM)
A separate 128 MB for pictures and textures.
Memory cleaner (GC)
Frees memory the game no longer uses. While it works the game can pause, so a lot of tricks make it faster or move it to another core.
Interpreter, AOT, JIT
Three ways to run the game’s code: the interpreter reads it line by line (slow); AOT turns it into fast machine code ahead of time on the Mac; the JIT does that while you play.
Cache
A saved copy of work already done, so it doesn’t have to be done again.
Card
The memory card. It reads only about 10 MB a second, so loading is mostly waiting on it.
Emulator (vlx), estimate
Our copy of the Vita that runs on the Mac. Numbers from it are estimates until the Vita measures them.
Matches the original
The rule over everything: the game must look and play exactly like the PC game. Anything different is an option you choose.

Kits 0–5 Getting it running

Done · measured on a real Vita
Kit 1 · Oct 8–9
Main Menu reached (text only): 442.8 ms (2.3 fps), boot ~6.1–6.3 min
Kit 2 · Oct 13
Menu on the Vita’s screen: 159.4 ms (6.3 fps), boot 3.6–5.0 min
Kit 3 · Oct 13
First playable menus: ~69 ms (~14.5 fps), boot ~3.9 min
Kit 4 · Oct 14
New Game reaches the opening quotes: 57.9 ms (17.3 fps), boot ~3.4 min
Kit 5 · Oct 15
LLVM test apps 51.4 ms (19.5 fps), boot 2.5–3.0 min, Settings in seconds
Memory
Room for the game: 144 MB in kit 1’s menu-only app, with only 11 MB left free; 124–125 MB in kits 3–5’s play apps (game AOT 117); the LLVM test apps +10 (134–138); the Main Menu holds ~72–76 MB of live data

32 tricks and items · tap one

  • Engine AOT−60–200 ms

    The engine's own code is turned into finished machine code on the Mac before it goes on the Vita, instead of being worked out while the game runs. The Vita has less to do, so things run faster.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−60–200 ms per frame

    A big share of kits 1-3's drop from 442.8 to ~69 ms per frame (measured as a whole, not per item)

  • Hop fix−1–5 ms

    A fix that cut out a wasted step every time the menu code jumped between the engine and the game. Small on its own, but it happens thousands of times a second.

    Helps withMenus & controls
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–5 ms per frame

    Small per call, but the menu makes thousands of these calls

  • Owner game AOT−2–6 ms

    The game's own code (not just the engine) is also turned into finished machine code ahead of time, in a special app only for the test Vita. The game's logic runs faster than when it is read line by line.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–6 ms per frame

    Game logic compiled ahead of time instead of interpreted (the game-AOT app)

  • LLVM (A/B apps 00191/00192, float-ABI + EH-VFP fixes)−10–13 ms

    LLVM is a smarter code builder. It makes the Vita's machine code smaller and faster. Two test apps compared it against the old builder, and two bugs it caused were fixed first.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    Frame time−10–13 ms per frame

    Kit 5's test apps: 64.5 → 51.4 ms at the idle menu (−20 %)

  • Delegate dispatch−0.5–2 ms

    Makes the way the game calls its 'do this when that happens' functions cheaper, and stops idle threads from wasting CPU time while they wait.

    Helps withMeasuring & testsSpeed (CPU)Menus & controls
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame
  • Lever 20 call-info cache−0.5–2 ms

    The runtime remembers how to make each kind of function call after the first time, instead of working it out again every time.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame
  • R2 SuppressGCTransition−0.5–2 ms

    Skips a slow safety handshake on calls into the engine that never need it. Every frame makes many of those calls, so it adds up.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame
  • Stage-2 engine speed pass−2–8 ms

    A round of speed-ups inside the engine: the hottest code paths found by measuring, then made cheaper one by one.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–8 ms per frame

    Several engine speed-ups together

  • Pooled snapshots−0.2–1 msHelps RAM

    Reuses the same blocks of memory for repeated work instead of asking for new memory each time. Less memory churn means fewer clean-up pauses.

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.2–1 ms per frame
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Fewer memory clean-ups

  • Retained UI pass−3–10 ms

    Menus that don't change are kept ready instead of being rebuilt every frame. The menu screen costs much less to draw.

    Helps withMenus & controls
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−3–10 ms per frame

    The menu stopped rebuilding every frame

  • Hotspots #4-#8−3–10 ms

    Hot spots number 4 to 8: the next five slowest parts of the engine, found by measuring, each made faster.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−3–10 ms per frame

    Five hot spots together

  • MT render prep−5–15 ms

    Getting the picture ready to draw is moved to its own CPU core. The main core then has more time for the game itself.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−5–15 ms per frame

    Picture preparation moved off the main core

  • GPU Stylizer−1–3 ms

    The game's 'Stylizer' colour effect is done by the graphics chip instead of the main processor. The picture looks the same and the CPU is freed up.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–3 ms per frame
  • Batcher−2–6 ms

    Groups many small things to draw into fewer big drawing jobs. The graphics chip has less overhead per frame.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–6 ms per frame
  • Half-res DXT pack textures−3–10 sVRAM +20–40 MB

    Textures are stored in a compressed format the Vita's graphics chip reads directly, at the size the Vita's screen actually needs. Less memory and faster loading.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)Boot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−3–10 s
    Video memory (VRAM)20–40 MB more free

    Less to read and less memory per texture

  • Near/far + frustum culling−1–4 ms

    Things that are off-screen (too close, too far, or outside the camera's view) are skipped instead of drawn.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–4 ms per frame
  • Diet + strict Vita pack−20–60 sRAM +10–30 MB

    The game's files are trimmed and repacked into one pack made just for the Vita. Smaller, and faster to read off the memory card.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−20–60 s
    RAM (main memory)10–30 MB more free

    Part of kits 1-5's boot going from ~6 to ~2.7 minutes

  • Boot GCHelps RAM

    One big memory clean-up right before the first frame, while the screen is still black. The game doesn't stutter for it later.

    Helps withMemoryBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Moves a ~1 s clean-up pause to the black screen before the first frame

  • IL poolRAM +5–10 MB

    A reserved area of memory where the game's code is loaded, so it doesn't break up the main memory into small pieces.

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    RAM (main memory)5–10 MB more free

    Less broken-up memory

  • B-1 icall bake−1–3 s

    The list of engine functions the game uses is worked out once on the Mac and saved, instead of being searched for at every boot.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−1–3 s

    Boot skips building the hook list

  • B-2 catalog bake−1–3 s

    The game's big asset list is pre-processed on the Mac and saved in a fast-to-read form. Boot skips that work.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−1–3 s

    Boot skips building the asset list

  • B-3 read-ahead−5–15 s

    Files are read from the card a little before they're needed, so the game waits less.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−5–15 s
  • B4 SerRules fast scan−0.5–2 s

    A faster way of checking how saved game data should be read back. Shaves time off loading.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−0.5–2 s
  • [Icall] hook-list cache−0.1–0.5 s

    The engine's list of hooks into the game is kept on the card after the first start, so later starts skip building it. Also: memory clean-ups happen during black screens, when nobody can see a pause.

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−0.1–0.5 s

    Hook list cached; clean-ups hidden in black frames

  • M2 pause-menu GC governorHelps RAM

    Memory clean-ups are timed to happen while the pause menu is up, so they don't interrupt play.

    Helps withMenus & controlsMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Clean-up pauses moved to the pause menu

  • 4th core−2–8 ms

    The Vita has a 4th CPU core that games normally can't use. With CapUnlocker we checked it works and gave it work to do.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Measuring & tests
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–8 ms per frame

    An extra core for background work

  • 444 MHz clocks−25 %

    The CPU is set to 444 MHz, the fastest speed the Vita allows apps. It had been starting at 333 MHz.

    Helps withBattery & clocks
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    Frame time−25 % frame time where the CPU is the limit

    333 → 444 MHz: about a quarter faster wherever the CPU is the limit

  • Shepaudio mixer + audio bake

    Our own sound system. It plays the game's sounds and music, which are converted once on the Mac into a format the Vita can play.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Makes sound work at all

  • P1 short-press latch

    A very quick button tap is caught even if it happens between two frames, so presses never get lost.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Controls: no lost taps

  • Display cap−1–5 ms

    The game is kept at the Vita's real screen size (960x544), so it never wastes effort on a bigger picture.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–5 ms per frame

    No drawing above the Vita's real resolution

  • Settings close−1–3 s

    Closing Settings got faster, by cutting repeated log writing and making a quicker copy of the menu objects.

    Helps withMenus & controlsMeasuring & tests
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−1–3 s

    Closing Settings faster

  • Touch screen as mouse

    Tapping the touch screen works like clicking a mouse, so the game's menus can be used by touch.

    Helps withMenus & controls
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Controls: touch works

  • Boot anatomy

    Boot breakdown: about 105 seconds before the logo can even show, 6 seconds of logo, about 33 seconds after. Most of the wait is before the logo, which is why kit 8 added the tiny launcher.

  • NGS CORRECTED 2026-10-21−0.6–0.7 ms

    The Vita's sound chip (NGS) can take most of the sound mixing off the CPU (about 60%). The first reading said it couldn't; that was wrong and got corrected.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Menus & controlsBoot & loading
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    Frame time−0.6–0.7 ms per frame · ≈ +0.4–0.5 fps at the menu

    About 60 % of sound mixing could leave the CPU (helper core, not the main core)

  • GPU transfer unit

    The Vita has a separate copy chip that rearranges and moves picture data about 100 times faster than the CPU. Texture loading moves to it.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)MemorySpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita

    About 100x faster texture rearranging than the CPU

  • Memory:

    Memory measured on the test Vita: the extra homebrew memory setting is on, about 199 MB of main memory free, 110 MB of video memory, and each memory miss costs about 252 nanoseconds.

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    A measurement

  • Storage:

    Card speed measured: about 11.6 MB per second when reading big chunks, much slower in small pieces. So the game should read big chunks.

    Helps withMemoryBoot & loading

  • Spine package−1–4 msRAM +3–10 MB

    A package of speed-ups for the animated characters (Spine): their skeleton data is prepared on the Mac ahead of time, poses are stored compressed, and the graphics chip helps bend the characters' shapes.

    Frame time−1–4 ms per frame · ≈ +0.7–2.8 fps at the menu
    RAM (main memory)3–10 MB more free

    Animated characters cheaper to update and store

    Checked by: baked-vs-live < 0.1 px every bone/frame (Mac); vlx identity; device A/B

  • Kept only where the shader's unpacking gives the same pose as the CPU path, else the decode cache

    The graphics chip only does that work where it gets exactly the same result as the CPU; anywhere else the normal way is kept, so nothing looks different.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Graphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Keeps it exact

    Checked by: baked-vs-live < 0.1 px every bone/frame (Mac); vlx identity; device A/B

  • Per-clip bounds−0.2–1 ms

    Each animation's biggest possible size is worked out ahead of time, so the engine can tell a character is off-screen without calculating its pose first.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess

    Skips characters off-screen earlier

Kit 8 Gameplay speed: physics, JIT, launchers

Done · measured on a real Vita
Idle menu
Measure 45.48 ms (22.0 fps); Play MT 40.15 ms (24.9 fps) at ~500 MHz
Boot
Measure 178.8 s · Play 152.7 s · Play MT 138.8 s (500)
Note
Launchers refused with 0x8002D003, fixed in 8.1
Memory
Room for the game: Play MT 135 MB, game AOT 117 (about the same); the intro walk needs ~225 MB against the ~157 the Vita gives (emulator estimate), so the memory work starts in kit 9

77 tricks and items · tap one

  • JIT helpers−3.8 ms

    Ways to make the JIT smarter. (A JIT turns game code into fast machine code while you play.) First: it also counts how long loops run, not just how often a function is called, so a big one-time loading loop gets sped up too.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Boot & loading
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    Frame time−3.8 ms per frame · ≈ +2.7 fps at the menu

    The JIT as a whole: game logic 21.9 → 18.2 ms on the test Vita (kit 9's version)

    Checked by: vlx identity interp vs JIT; device compile time per scene, code-space use

  • (2) first-use order−0.5–2 s

    The functions the game needed first last time get compiled first this time, so they're ready when needed.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−0.5–2 s

    Hot code ready sooner after boot

    Checked by: vlx identity interp vs JIT; device compile time per scene, code-space use

  • (3) a 'not worth it' list−0.2–1 s

    Functions that got compiled but barely ran are skipped next time. Less wasted work, and more room in the JIT's small memory space.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Stability & safetyMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−0.2–1 s

    Less compiling

    Checked by: vlx identity interp vs JIT; device compile time per scene, code-space use

  • (4) direct calls−0.2–0.8 ms

    Compiled code calls other compiled code directly, without a middleman step in between.

Kit 9 Runtime + memory pack

Done · measured on a real Vita
Idle menu
Measure 39.62 ms (25.2 fps); Play MT 35.98 ms (27.8 fps) at ~500 MHz
Boot
Measure 164.5 s · Play 138.8 s · Play MT 126.8 s (500)
JIT
Game logic −17 % (kit 8’s JIT −13 %)
Memory
Room for the game: Play MT 135 → 140 MB (+5), game AOT 117 → 126 (+9), from smaller apps
Logos
Launcher logo at ~0.6 s with picture and sound

37 tricks and items · tap one

  • R1 fast TLS−0.5–2.4 ms

    Each CPU thread keeps a few private values it looks up constantly. Kit 8 searched a list every time; now it reads them straight from a fixed spot in the processor. That lookup was about 9% of the main thread's time at the menu.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Frame time−0.5–2.4 ms per frame · ≈ +0.3–1.6 fps at the menu

    Emulator, scaled to the test Vita. Kit 9 as a whole: idle menu 45.48 → 39.62 ms (−5.9 ms) measured

  • Lever 10 typed icall stubs + E5 zero-hop mirrors−0.3–1 ms

    The game's calls into the engine skip a slow 'figure out the arguments' step, and the most common 'what's the value of X' questions are answered instantly from a copy kept ready.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Measuring & testsGameplay
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Frame time−0.3–1 ms per frame · ≈ +0.2–0.7 fps at the menu
  • R5 profile-ordered AOT layout−0–2 ms

    The compiled code is arranged so functions that run together sit together in memory, which the Vita's small CPU cache likes.

Kit 10 Loading

Done · measured on a real Vita
Boot
Play MT 104.5 s from 126.8 (−18 %) · Play 106.7 s (−23 %) · game AOT 111.6 s (−24 %) · Measure 136.8 s (−17 %) · launchers 120–122 s; the first start after installing does a one-time 11.5 s file check
Screen
The loading screen instead of black (80 s, then 61 s on the second start); both logos all 188 frames
Frame rate
Idle menu 39.98 ms (25.0 fps): about the same as kit 9, as planned
Memory
The apps use 4–7 MB less of their own memory; walk 213.8 MiB (emulator); room for the game: Play MT 140 MB, game AOT 127 (about the same: the shared pool comes in kit 11)
Card
Pack 182 → 39.5 MiB; Verify Files OK at 5.7 MiB/s
Settings
Opens in 6.2 s, closes in 3.3 s (kit 9: 5.8 / 4.3)
Float rules
The PC’s float rules cost nothing on the Vita (×1.00) and pass New Game; still off until a physics-heavy test
Extras
Learned AOT −3.7 s boot; the new pack is worth ~3.2 s on your card; DMA copies 22 % faster with 0 errors; the shared memory pool passed and gave the game +13 MB more room (153 vs 140 MB)

26 tricks and items · tap one

  • E3 field programs−23 s

    When a scene loads, Unity reads every object's data the slow, general-purpose way. This makes a ready-made 'recipe' for each kind of object once, so loading just follows the recipe. The biggest single speed-up for boot and loading.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−23 s

    Boot −18 % on the emulator; ~126.8 → ~104 s on Play MT estimated. Main Menu scene load −44 %, New Game load −40 %

  • Asset object snapshots−1–2 s

    Big data that never changes (all the game's text, the item and doctrine tables) is stored already in the shape the game uses, so loading copies it in instead of rebuilding it.

    Helps withMatches the originalBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−1–2 s

    Text table 976 → 132 ms, controller maps 1342 → 315 ms (emulator, part of the boot gain)

  • B-4 decode-ahead worker + LZ4 records−1–4 sCard −142 MB

    Compression makes loading faster on the Vita: the card is slow (~10 MB/s) but a spare core unpacks quickly. Each file gets the best format, unpacked in the background before it's needed.

    Helps withBoot & loadingSpeed (CPU)Memory
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−1–4 s
    Card space142 MB smaller on the card

    Pack 182 → 39.5 MB on the card; boot reads 54 → 12 MB

  • Dependency-parallel Addressables loads−0.5–2 s

    When something needs several pieces, they're read and unpacked side by side instead of one after another.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−0.5–2 s
  • SceDmac DMA bulk copies−0.5–2 s

    Big copies are done by the Vita's copy hardware (DMA) instead of the CPU, about twice as fast.

    Helps withBoot & loadingMeasuring & testsGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    Boot / loading−0.5–2 s

    Copies ~2x faster than the CPU (kit 7 probe)

  • Pack-time visit-order layout−0.5–2 s

    Files in the pack are arranged in the order the game reads them, so the card reads in long straight runs.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−0.5–2 s

    Part of the pack-layer time −47 %

  • Learning caches on the card−2–6 s

    The Vita keeps notes on the card about what each start needed, so the next start can prepare it early. It gets faster the more you play. There's a size limit you choose in Settings, and only results are stored, never game data.

    Helps withBoot & loadingStability & safetySpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−2–6 s

    Second start: 35 of 35 prefetches used (emulator); the device gain is measured for the first time in kit 10

  • Pack-time preload manifests−1–4 s

    For each predictable moment (New Game, Continue, menus), the exact files needed are listed ahead of time and stored together, so preparing them is one straight read.

    Helps withBoot & loadingGraphics (GPU)Menus & controls
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    Boot / loading−1–4 s
  • Idle-window scheduler−1–4 s

    Logos, quotes, loading screens and fades are dead time. The engine uses them to prepare upcoming data (never running game code early).

    Helps withMeasuring & testsBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Boot / loading−1–4 s

    Work done during logos, quotes and fades

  • Loading screen−1–3 s

    The loading screen itself is cheap to draw; the real cost is what happens behind it. While it shows, the engine gives loading more time each frame so loads finish sooner.

    Helps withBoot & loadingMenus & controls
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Boot / loading−1–3 s

    Loads finish sooner behind the loading screen

  • Memory pressure ladderHelps RAM

Tried, turned off

Probes

32 tricks and items · tap one

  • The shared pool by defaultRAM +13 MB

    The C memory and the game's memory share one block, so savings anywhere become room for the game. Tested on the test Vita in kit 10 (X2): it passed and gave the game 13 MB more room. Now it becomes the default.

    Helps withMemoryStability & safety
    Estimated effectMeasured on a real Vita
    RAM (main memory)13 MB more free

    Measured on a real Vita: 153 MB of room vs 140

  • Engine records dietRAM +15–25 MB

    The engine's own records for every object get slimmer: rarely used parts move out, and data only needed while loading is dropped afterwards. Target: 15 to 25 MB saved.

    Helps withMemoryGameplayBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    RAM (main memory)15–25 MB more free
  • CompactionRAM +5.6 MB

    The memory cleaner packs objects together to close gaps (built in kit 10, opt-in). On by default if the Vita test shows the pauses stay OK. About 5.6 MB.

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)5.6 MB more free

    On the walk; longer clean-up pauses (about 2.7x)

  • The C heap's own censusRAM +5–20 MB

    Look at exactly what fills the C side's ~100 MB (loaded code, type information, the pack index, audio, physics) and cut the biggest pieces without changing anything.

    Helps withMemoryMeasuring & testsBoot & loading
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    RAM (main memory)5–20 MB more free

  • The intro's load peakRAM +5–20 MB

    Helps withBoot & loadingGameplayStability & safety
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate

  • Logging diet tested on the Vita: boot ~2–3 s faster−2–3 s

    Today the game's log is written to the memory card one line at a time, every frame. A switch built in kit 8 writes it once per frame instead, and keeps every line in the crash recorder. Measured on a real Vita (1 Nov, corrected 4 Nov): about 2–3 s less boot after the engine starts, 3.2 s in all in one Measure pair; the idle menu frame unchanged (37.02 -> 36.97 ms), because the menu writes few log lines. An earlier '4.7 s' mixed two different boot measures. Release builds keep it on.

  • Player build: test counters off−0.5–2 ms

    The test kits count and record a lot every frame: every engine call, frame statistics, stack use, GC pauses, and a crash file written to the card every 5 seconds. A player build keeps only the crash recorder and switches the rest off. (added 31 Oct: more ms, looks identical)

    Helps withMeasuring & tests
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame · ≈ +0.3–1.3 fps at the menu
  • Test whether the GPU does the maths bit-for-bit like the CPU

    A small test runs the exact same follower-vertex maths on the CPU and on the GPU over millions of inputs and compares every bit. If they match, the GPU can take over placing follower vertices without changing a single pixel. If not, it stays on the CPU. (1 Nov)

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Measuring & testsGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Decides two kit 13 items

  • Experiment: measure the shared cache and a helper core

    The Vita's CPU cores share a second-level cache whose size isn't published. A test measures it, then tries something almost never done in a game: a spare core runs a few steps ahead of the main core just reading data, so it's already in the shared cache when the main core needs it. This CPU has no automatic prefetching, so a miss costs a long wait. (1 Nov)

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Measuring & tests
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Decides the helper-core item in kit 13

  • Test the Vita's hidden media cores (Venezia)

    The plugin that lets us use the Vita's media processor is already installed. A small test app checks it's loaded, sends an unpacking job to the media cores, checks the answer is byte-for-byte identical to the main CPU's, times it on one core, two cores and all of them, measures how fast data moves in and out, and runs once while a video plays (Sony's video and audio share those cores). The answer decides how kit 13 builds the compressed RAM. (moved up 1 Nov)

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)Measuring & tests
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Decides the kit 15 job

Kit 12 Menus, boot and memory

Done · measured on a real Vita
Status
Ran on a real Vita 5 Nov, every step and extra (all PASS)
Cleanup
Leaving the menu frees 8.5–10 MB of game memory on the black frames (~1 s pause each); the boot’s two cleanups free ~4 MB each but cost ~3.6 s of boot: kit 13 keeps the menu exit and skips the boot
Memory
Room for the game: Play MT launcher 158 MB (+5), Play MT 156 (+3), Play 155 (+7), game AOT 148 (+2), game-AOT launcher 146 (+2); the menus’ peak 97.5–98.5 MB (kit 11 100.6); the Main Menu’s live data 60.4 MB (−2.8)
Idle menu

Boot
To the menu, warm starts: Measure ~107 s (kit 11 103), game AOT ~104.5 s (kit 11 98), launchers ~101 s (same); compressed code costs ~0.5 s (a tie) for 3.5 MB
Not yet

Extras
Compaction back at 66 %: boot +2.5 s (90 % stays on); game code in video memory: 172 MB of room (+24) but boot +11 s; recorded UI commands: no gain

22 tricks and items · tap one

  • A lighter asset indexRAM +3.4–6 MB

    The game's list of every asset in a scene file used to be built as one object per entry (about 148,000 of them in the intro). Now each entry is read straight out of the file's own table, so those objects never exist. Built in kit 12, on by default; the Vita run checks it.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)3.4–6 MB more free

    Emulator: −5.65 MiB of live data on the walk, the game's room +3–4 MB at the menu; costs ~0.15–0.19 s of Main Menu loading

  • The game's code read compressed from the appRAM +3.5 MB

    The code files inside the app are stored compressed and unpacked only when a method first runs, so less of them sits in main memory. Built in kit 12, on by default; steps 8–11 of the Vita run compare it with the old way.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)3.5 MB more free

    Emulator: code in main memory −3.5 MiB on the walk (−3.3 at the menu); boot cost estimated at a few tenths of a second

  • No line-number tables for interpreted codeRAM +1.2 MB

    The runtime no longer keeps a table mapping running code back to source lines. Nothing on screen changes; crash logs just lose the line offsets (the method names stay). Built in kit 12, on by default.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)1.2 MB more free

    Emulator: −1.2 MiB on the walk, −0.8 MiB at the Main Menu

  • The menu's leftovers cleaned up on black loading framesRAM +5 MB

    When you start a game, the Main Menu's leftover memory used to stay around while the next scene loaded. Now a clean-up runs at every scene change, only while the screen is black, so nobody sees the pause. Built in kit 12, on by default; steps 13–16 of the Vita run measure it.

Tried on a real Vita in kit 11 (an extra step): no cost, and the boot came out 2.4 s faster in that one run. On by default in kit 12.

22 tricks and items · tap one

  • Keep exactly what the game still uses: Unity's real 'unload unused assets'Card −25–40 MB

    The owner's idea (5 Nov): the intro keeps exactly what it needs and lets go of the rest. The game already asks for this: at every scene change, on the black frames, it calls Unity's 'unload unused assets', which in Unity frees every asset nothing references any more. Shepherd's version only runs a garbage sweep there, so loaded assets pile up from screen to screen (the Main Menu's into the quotes and the intro, the base's into the dungeon). Measured during the quotes (Mac, Vita sizes): the game's own code can still reach ~42 MB; ~40–45 MB more of real objects is kept alive only by Shepherd. Doing what Unity does there frees that, exactly: anything the game asks for again is read from the card again, as Unity reloads it from disk. Data the game still holds but won't use soon (other languages' text, follower looks nobody wears) can sleep packed until touched. Owner 5 Nov: at every transition, not just the intro. Every loading screen you can't back out of (the base to a dungeon, a floor to the boss, back to the base) cleanses the previous room's leftover assets; dungeon rooms you can walk back to are switched off by the game, so they sleep packed and wake when you return.

    Helps withMatches the originalBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    Card space25–40 MB smaller on the card

    Built, off by default: the release frees ~1.7 MB at the quotes and ~2.3 MB in the intro (Mac); worth far more later at base ↔ dungeon (~90 MB of the dungeon’s numbers are the base’s leftovers)

  • Cleanups only where they pay: skip the boot's scene changes−3–4 s

    Kit 12's step 13–16 A/B on the Vita: the menu-exit cleanup frees 8.5–10 MB on the black frames, but the boot's two cleanups free only ~4 MB each and cost ~3.6 s of boot. Keep the menu exit and the heavy scene changes, skip the boot chain.

    Helps withStability & safetyBoot & loading

    Kit 12 device A/B: Main Menu at ~101.8 s with the boot cleanups vs ~98.2 s without

  • Kit 12's new shaders saved in the shader cache−2–3 s

    The round-mask program and the three loading-screen look programs are compiled on every start (~0.31 s at boot, ~2.3 s when New Game starts) because they aren't in the saved shader cache or the boot set. Add them.

  • UI canvases laid out only when something on them changes (Unity's rule)−1–4 ms

    Every follower carries small UI canvases (thought bubbles, warnings, prompts). Unity rebuilds a canvas only when something on it changes; Shepherd's layout pass touches every canvas every frame. Measured 5 Nov on the Mac: of the time 99 extra followers add, 57 % is this layout pass. Doing it Unity's way is exact and makes idle bubbles cost almost nothing.

  • Shelf sweep: everything built but not in use gets adopted, re-tested or dropped

    The 'Built, not in use' tab lists every feature, test and probe result that exists but isn't working for the kits yet (25 on 5 Nov). Kit 13 takes the ones marked for it: the re-tests (kit 11's GPU rows with their slow call fixed, the memory-cleaner variants, UI replay, JIT shortcuts, learned AOT, canvas placement), the tests never run (the follower sweep, the decoder checks, first-call traces, picture checks against the PC, the full-length checks), the probe wins to wire in (helper-core prefetch, compressed RAM) and the half-built items (real asset unloading, smaller headers, Verify Files over code files, re-keyed lists, cached video memory). Each one ends the kit adopted, re-tested with a number, or dropped with the reason. Nothing stays in limbo.

  • The idle menu profiled on the Vita itself

    No fps gain landed in kits 10–12 (the idle menu went 37.7 -> ~40 ms). On the Vita the menu frame is ~19 ms of drawing and ~19–21 ms of logic, and the Mac can't say what that logic is (its version is tiny). One Measure step with kit 7's profiler on gives the per-system split on the real hardware, so the next fps work aims at what actually costs time.

  • The round masks fixed and made cheaper, still exact−1–2 ms

    Kit 12's round masks broke two masked panels on the Vita: the Settings panel's background went missing (with a yellow tint) and the Verify Files confirmation drew purple bands (reproduced on the Mac: masks off alone fixes Settings). Fix: draw the mask's own picture whenever the game asks for it to show, then check every masked screen against the PC. They also cost ~2 ms per menu frame (37.9 ms off vs ~39.9 on): drawing the mask work only where a mask actually is, with its program in the shader cache, should give most of that back.

  • Boot loading screen: the emblems crossfade into each other

    The owner's idea (5 Nov): instead of one random emblem for the whole boot, the boot loading screen fades from one emblem to the next at a set pace (suggested: about 8 s each, a 1 s crossfade, shuffled without repeats), using only the emblems allowed for the save (co-op, Woolhaven and the other sets follow the same rules). Checked on the kit 12 recordings: every recording has the same 196 frames at 30 fps, and at the same frame every pixel outside the centre emblem is identical (the spinning ring in the corner included), while the emblem itself is a still picture. So only the emblem's box blends and the ring never jumps. Built as one shared recording + one small still per emblem, it also uses less card space. The game's own in-game loading screens stay as the original (one random emblem per load).

Planned · not built yet

On the Mac emulator at the Vita’s memory layout, Play MT walks the intro, kneels, dies, makes the deal, is reborn and fights the first room in about 2 runs of 3 (the others run out of memory late in the fight).   • The interpreter’s per-method data stored smallerHelps RAM

    The code interpreter keeps working data for every game function it has prepared. During the intro’s load that data is a big share of the memory left outside the game’s objects. Storing it smaller, with the exact same behaviour, gives room back. (Added 6 Nov from kit 13’s walk findings.)

    The code interpreter stops keeping a debug table for every function it prepares. Only error stack traces read it, so the game behaves exactly the same; the engine's error lines lose one detail. Built in kit 12, opt-in until now: on by default for the walk, with an off switch. (Added 6 Nov.)

  • The intro made ready during the quotes

    The owner's idea (5 Nov), building on kit 8's head start (which already reads up to 16 MB of the next scene's file data into spare video memory during idle moments): while the quotes play, also unpack and parse the intro's data into its ready-to-build form in the background, so the load frame only builds objects. Data only: no game code runs early and objects are still made at the original moment. Done after the menu's assets are let go, it shortens the load and can lower its memory peak (the parsing garbage is cleaned up in pieces during the quotes instead of piling up in the load frame). (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withGeneral

  • Smaller object headersRAM +10–11 MB

    Every object carries a small hidden header. Making it 4 bytes smaller across 2.8 million objects saves about 10 to 11 MB. (Moved from kit 11, not built there; 4 Nov.) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

  • A compact store for the templates the game keeps

    The game keeps a template of every prefab it may spawn (93,263 records at the intro). They sit in memory at full size though most are only read when something spawns. A compact store keeps them smaller and hands the game exactly the same data. (Added 6 Nov from kit 13’s walk findings.)

  • Experiment: compressed RAM for cold dataRAM +10–40 MB

    Data that's in RAM but not being used right now (other areas' assets, closed menus, other languages, audio not playing) is kept squeezed in RAM and unpacked by a spare core just before it's needed, much faster than reloading from the card. Nothing is lost in the squeeze. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Base scenes

  • Room in the apps for the native code

    The app's own code is loaded into the same memory the game runs in, and the Vita hands that memory out in whole 1 MB steps, so code that pushes an app past a step costs the game 1 MB of room. Native C is usually much smaller than the compiled C# it replaces. The plan: both versions stay in the app while the Vita's A/B run decides (the off switch), then the C# copy is left out of the Vita apps; the Mac keeps it as the reference the lockstep check compares against. Kit 13's one copy of shared generic code (5.8 MB measured) and trimmed library code make room first. Every build is measured against its step.

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Keeps every app inside its memory step; Play has ~0.1-0.2 MB of code room left after kit 12

  • Sleeping objects: packed until the game touches them (dormant subtrees)

    Objects nobody is using stay in a small packed form and wake the instant the game touches one; every touch goes through Shepherd's own code, so the game never notices (the owner's idea, 5 Nov, matches this plan).   • L6 dormant inactive subtreesRAM +2–8 MB

    Objects switched off for a long time are kept packed, to save memory. (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    RAM (main memory)2–8 MB more free
  • The intro's two halves shelved in turn: the afterlife room packed while the forest plays, the forest packed in the afterlifeRAM +1–2 MB

    The owner's idea (5 Nov). The intro scene holds both places: the forest and the One Who Waits' room. At the sacrifice the game switches the forest off and the afterlife on, and after the crown and the intro video it switches them back. Switched-off objects stop updating and drawing, but stay in memory in full, so the whole afterlife room sits in memory, switched off, through the forest walk and the first fight, exactly where the Vita runs out. Packing a part the game has switched off, and unpacking it when the game switches it back on (during its own black transitions or the video, so nobody waits), gives that memory to what is on screen. The game decides when; Shepherd only stores it smaller. (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.) (6 Nov: kit 13 measured it small for the intro: ~1–2 MB; moved to the base, where 64 % of objects are switched off.)

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)1–2 MB more free

    Kit 13 (Mac): the room's engine-side data is ~1–2 MB; the rest is the game's own objects, which can't be packed exactly

  • The base's script diet: nothing recomputed that can't have changed

    The owner's rule (5 Nov): if something has a fixed layout and habit unless something touches it, don't keep checking it. Measured at the base (Mac): ~400 script calls per frame, mostly living decoration. Plan, each exact: (1) the lighting helpers (78 calls) and other scripts that usually find nothing changed are skipped until something they read changes (the guard watch); (2) the grass (12) and anything else that follows a fixed pattern from time and position is computed in one native pass with the same maths in the same order, and skipped while off screen where its effect is only the picture; (3) the animation detail managers (98) run as one native pass; (4) the critters (48) keep running, because they move and use the game's random numbers, which must stay in the same order, but in one batch; (5) the frosted UI panels redraw their blur only when what's behind them changes, and the HUD only when its values change. Reacting to the player (grass bending when walked through, critters fleeing) always runs normally.

    Helps withStability & safetyGameplay
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac

    Mac profile 5 Nov: the base's scripts are ~0.6 ms of an ~8 ms Mac frame (~7 %); the device profile in kit 13 shows their real weight on the Vita

  • Skip per-frame script calls that can't do anything yet (guard watch)

    Many per-frame scripts, followers' especially, begin with a check ('a bubble to show?', 'snowing?', 'freezing?'). When none of the values that first check reads has changed, the rest can't run: the engine watches those values and skips the call until one changes. Exact. Measured 5 Nov on the Mac: ~400 script calls per frame at the base with one follower, ~2,900 with 100 (about 25 per follower).

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac

    Mac profile 5 Nov: ~25 per-frame calls per follower; the share this removes is measured in kit 16

  • Garbage collector tuned for the big base−0.5–2 msRAM +0.6 MB

    RAM (main memory)0.6 MB more free

    Base scenes: shorter GC pauses

  • The empty base as low as it can go

    The base with no followers is made as cheap as possible while staying identical to the original. About 12 ms a frame is the ceiling 60 fps needs, not the goal: every millisecond under it is room for followers. The follower sweep measures it first and splits it up (buildings' scripts, drawing, lighting and weather, menus, sound) so each fix aims at a measured share. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withGameplay
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Sets the bar for 60 fps at the base

  • Measure the re-decide frames, then keep the job list

    About every 2nd or 3rd frame at a big base, someone re-decides, and the game rebuilds the whole list of jobs on offer by asking every building, then matches jobs to followers. First measure how big those spikes are; then, if proven identical, keep the job list until a building's offer changes. Good sign: no building's offer draws a random number. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withMeasuring & tests
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Measurement first; the fix only if proven exact

  • Base answers prepared during the loading screen

    While the loading screen plays, the CPU mostly waits on the memory card. That time fills the base's remembered answers (beds, building lists, countdowns) so the first frames at the base don't stutter. Same answers, just ready sooner. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

26 tricks and items · tap one

  • The first dungeon on the device. Test

    The first dungeon running on the test Vita, checked for no loading hitches during fights.

    Helps withGameplayMeasuring & testsStability & safety
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    The goal: the first dungeon on the test Vita

  • Trigger checks only between nearby colliders (a grid), callbacks in the original order

    From the owner's find (a reel on the Hitman ragdoll trick, 5 Nov): Shepherd's trigger system tests every moving collider against every live one, which grows with followers x buildings at a busy base. Splitting the world into a grid so each collider only checks its neighbours' cells gives the same overlaps with far fewer checks; exact as long as the shape tests and the order of the enter / stay / exit callbacks stay as they are.

    Helps withMeasuring & testsMatches the original
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac

    Scales with the base: the saving grows with followers and buildings (measured first in kit 13-14)

  • The static world baked into lookup grids (a distance-field-style cache)

    The other half of the same trick: questions about things that never move (the base's outline check every follower runs, tests against static colliders) answered from a precomputed grid: cells fully inside or outside answer at once, and only cells on an edge run the exact test, so every answer is identical.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac

    Exact by construction: only the boundary cells run the original test

  • Code layout by call graph−0.3–1.5 ms

    Functions that call each other are placed next to each other in memory, so the Vita's small instruction cache misses less.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)

  • Small-vs-fast code per method−0.5–2 ms

    Each busy function is built both small and fast, both are timed on the test Vita, and the winner is kept. On a cache-starved CPU, smaller often wins (the Super Mario 64 lesson).

    Helps withMatches the originalMeasuring & testsSpeed (CPU)

25 tricks and items · tap one

  • Paths reused while the map is unchanged−1–4 ms

    Followers walk the same routes over and over. A path between the same two points on an unchanged map is the same answer, so it's reused until something changes where they can walk. (31 Oct) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–4 ms per frame

    At a busy base

  • Buildings' thinking skipped while nothing changed−0.5–2 ms

    The follower idea applied to buildings, farm plots, beds, shrines, kitchens and decorations: each one's script only runs again when something it looked at has changed. (1 Nov) Most buildings in the game already only react to the time of day changing, so this mostly helps the few that update every frame. (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame

    At a large base

  • The bed search kept until beds change−2–15 ms

    Every frame, every sleeping follower without a bed searches for a free one, and each search checks every bed against every follower. At a big base at night that can be about 100,000 checks a frame. The answer only changes when a bed or someone's home changes, so it's kept and reused until then. (task study, 1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–15 ms per frame

    Big base at night; depends on how many lack a bed

  • Building lists kept until buildings change−0.2–1 ms

    Some follower tasks ask for 'all buildings of this kind' every frame, and the game builds a brand-new list each time by checking every building. The list is kept until a building is added or removed. (task study, 1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withStability & safety
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.2–1 ms per frame

    Plus less memory churn

  • Random numbers without the garbage−0.1–1 msHelps RAM

    Every random number the game asks for (there are 3,878 places that ask) currently packs its inputs into a new little box and unpacks the answer, leaving about four scraps of memory behind each time. A direct path gives the exact same numbers with no scraps. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withMemory
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.1–1 ms per frame
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Plus fewer memory clean-up pauses; busy base

  • Saving the random state without the slow lookup

    When the game saves and restores its random-number state (seasons, enemy encounter rolls), our engine looks the fields up by name every time. Reading them directly gives the same values. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess

    A few microseconds, only on those events; removes a small hitch

  • Re-deciding followers: only look where someone is re-deciding−0.3–1 ms

    Every frame the game checks all 98 places a follower can belong to, and for each place walks through every follower to see if anyone wants a new job. At 100 followers that's 9,800 checks a frame, even when nobody does. One pass finds who wants a new job and where, and only those places are visited, in the same order. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withGameplay
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.3–1 ms per frame

    Every frame at a 100-follower base

  • Animation lookups remembered, no throwaway text−0.1–0.3 ms

    Every time any character changes animation, the game's animation library finds it by name and then makes two throwaway lowercase copies of the name, even though the answer is always the same. A name it doesn't have makes it throw an error, catch it, and search the whole list. Each name's answer is remembered instead. Same results, no scraps, no errors. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withMenus & controlsGameplay
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.1–0.3 ms per frame

    Busy base; more if missing names come up every frame

  • Skin, bone and slot lookups remembered

    The game finds follower outfits, bones and slots by searching lists by name (over 250 places in the code ask). Each answer is remembered until the list changes. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess

  • Follower vertices placed by the GPU (if the test passes)−2–6 msRAM +2–3 MB

    The CPU keeps working out each follower's bones exactly as now (game code reads them), but the GPU places every vertex from those bones, colours them with the same exact values, and keeps the rest in its own buffers shared by every follower wearing the same look. Only built if the kit 11 test shows identical results. (1 Nov) (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

  • Still parts of the base drawn once and reused while nothing changes

    The owner's idea (5 Nov): while the camera stands still, keep the parts of the base that didn't change as a ready picture and draw only what moves (followers, the Lamb, effects) over it. Exact only where pixels really stay the same: today just 10.8 % of the base's screen is identical from one frame to the next with the Lamb standing still, because the light shifts and the grass and torches move. First measure how the game's light changes (in steps or every frame), then build it where it is exact. (Moved from kit 13, not built there; 6 Nov.)

  • Memory: HOT per-frame follower data is packed tight but never compressedRAM +5–15 MB

    RAM (main memory)5–15 MB more free
  • The save format never changes−2–6 ms

    The save format never changes. Follower data is rearranged into compact, cache-friendly arrays inside the engine.

    Helps withStability & safetySpeed (CPU)Gameplay
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    Frame time−2–6 ms per frame

    At a big base

  • Follower thinking skipped while nothing changed−2–10 ms

    A follower's brain runs every frame, and its needs grow a tiny bit every frame, so a plain 'nothing changed' check would never fire. The trick splits the cheap part from the expensive part: the needs keep growing every frame with the exact same maths, but the decisions (what to feel, what to do next) are skipped until a need can actually cross a line that matters, worked out ahead from its rate. Random rolls always run on schedule. (the owner's idea, 1 Nov) From the task study: 124 of the 129 tasks that run every frame qualify. Only 5 roll a random number every single frame (dissenting, ill, and scared/running-away followers) and always have to run.

    Helps withStability & safetyGameplay
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−2–10 ms per frame

    At a busy base; depends on how much is thinking

  • The self-checking recording−1–5 ms

    A follower in a repeating routine (walk, work, walk back) has its steps recorded and reused, but only while each step's inputs still match. The moment anything differs, like the player, the weather or another follower, it goes back to thinking normally from that exact moment. (the owner's idea, 1 Nov)

    Helps withMeasuring & tests
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–5 ms per frame

    At a busy base

  • Movement caught up only when looked at (research)−1–4 ms

    Followers walking off screen aren't moved every frame while nothing looks at them. When something does, they're caught up step by step with the same maths, landing exactly where they'd have been. (the owner's idea, 1 Nov)

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−1–4 ms per frame

    At a busy base

  • Thought expiry by its next time−0.5–2 ms

    Every frame each follower checks every one of its thoughts to see if any ran out. Instead it keeps the soonest expiry time, and one comparison a frame replaces the whole check; the thought still expires on exactly the same frame. (1 Nov)

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame

    At a busy base

  • Follower countdowns kept in one packed list

    18 follower tasks (idle, fake work, fake leisure and more) only roll a random number when a countdown runs out. Their countdowns are kept in one tight list and ticked down the exact same way every frame; a task only runs on the frame its countdown hits zero, in the original order, so every random number comes out the same. (1 Nov)

    Helps withGameplay
    Estimated effectRough guess

    Lets these 18 join the skip below

  • Performance mode: the base drawn as a looping set of images while you stand still

    The owner's idea (5 Nov), as an option you choose, not the default: while the camera stands still, the base's background is drawn from a few high-quality images that loop, with followers, the Lamb and effects drawn live on top. It looks very slightly different from the PC (the light drifts through the day and the grass and torches don't share one loop), and it does nothing while you walk (the camera moves, so everything shifts).

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)Gameplay
    Estimated effectRough guess

    Rough guess: standing-still frames several times faster (~100 ms -> 10-20 ms of drawing on the Vita), walking unchanged; ~2-16 MB of video memory, no main memory saved

  • Lifecycle attribute caching

    Caching a certain kind of game setup data: dropped, because it would run game code early.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect
  • Fidelity rejects

    Tricks that change how the game plays or looks (slower physics, fewer particles, capping followers, lower resolution by default): dropped under 'match the original'.

    Helps withMatches the originalGameplayGraphics (GPU)
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Would change how the game plays or looks

  • True zeros (kits 5-10)

    Pre-baked font glyphs (no glyph was ever missing), per-scene memory arenas (nothing to free) and packed renderer records (already covered): there's nothing there to take.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Nothing to gain

  • Overlaps (kits 5-10)

    Flattened load lists (kit 10's object programs already cut that work) and quickening (the JIT already compiles the busy code): they go after work that's already been cut.

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Already covered

  • Impossible or obsolete (kits 5-10)

    Pure JIT (the Vita has no Mono JIT), zstd dictionaries (zstd isn't used), the old A/B apps, unbroken files, DMA reads from video memory, the old G7 buffer test (replaced) and the audio chip offload (the mixer is already on another core).

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect
  • Against the rules (kits 5-10)

    Compiling scenes or combat code ahead (it would run game code early), GPU-skinned poses (can't be exact), reading inside your saves, the live Wi-Fi timeline (the game apps stay offline) and untried memory types (could hang the system).

    Helps withGeneral
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

    Fidelity and safety rules

  • Depth copy moved to where it's needed−0.2–1 ms

    A depth-copy step moved to the spot where it doesn't interrupt the chip's tiles. Dropped (kit 11 found it doesn't apply): the engine makes no depth copy and has no depth texture, so there is nothing to move.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)Speed (CPU)Measuring & tests
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.2–1 ms per frame
  • Whole-pixel UI scroll shift−0.5–2 ms

    When a scrolling list moves by whole pixels, the already-drawn part is shifted and only the new strip is drawn (an old PC game trick). Dropped for now (kit 12): shifting the scroll list by whole pixels is not exact on the scroll lists we can reach, so it would change the picture.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)Menus & controlsStability & safety
    Estimated effectRough guess
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame

    While lists scroll

  • SkeletonUtilityBone batch−0.5–2 ms

    Copying animation bone positions to objects as one fast native loop. Dropped as its own row (21 Oct research): in this game the SkeletonUtilityBone copies return immediately, so there is nothing to batch; the real per-frame work is the followers' bone helpers, which kit 15's native bone-helper pass covers.

    Helps withGraphics (GPU)Matches the originalMemory
    Estimated effectRoadmap estimate
    Frame time−0.5–2 ms per frame
  • Loaded asset files let go once the scene is builtHelps RAM

    After the base loads, Shepherd still keeps data for 268 asset files it no longer needs to read (all marked releasable). Letting go of them right after the load frees that memory; first check how much of it the Vita's own pack still keeps. Dropped after kit 13’s Mac measurement (6 Nov): what stays after the load is file bookkeeping, not data buffers, so there is nothing worth letting go.

    Helps withBoot & loading
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Mac test 5 Nov: ~31 MB of loaded file data kept after the base loads (the Vita's pack may keep less)

  • Only the current language's text kept readyHelps RAM

    The game's text manager keeps every language loaded (about 22 MB of data reachable from it at the base on the Mac). Keeping the other languages compressed until someone switches language frees most of it, exactly. Dropped after kit 13’s Mac measurement (6 Nov): the card already carries only the current language (the other 20 would cost ~18.5 MB at the base). Switching languages on the Vita is a separate question: it needs the other languages in the pack.

    Helps withMenus & controls
    Estimated effectEmulator / Mac
    RAM (main memory)helps (no number yet)

    Mac test 5 Nov: ~22 MB reachable from the text manager; the share that is other languages still to be measured

  • Recorded menu commands (UI replay)

    Off: it can't run together with kit 12's round masks yet. Dropped from the “Built, not in use” list in kit 13 (6 Nov): kit 12’s device X3 showed no gain with the masks off (38.15 vs 37.93 ms).

    Helps withMenus & controls
    Estimated effectNo speed effect
  • JIT getter shortcuts

    Built in kit 9, left off. Dropped from the “Built, not in use” list in kit 13 (6 Nov): kit 13 found 0 places where they compile.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectNo speed effect
  • Learned AOT on the other apps

    Only the two game-AOT apps use the play-learned compile list. Dropped from the “Built, not in use” list in kit 13 (6 Nov): kit 13’s emulator check: 3 fewer slow-path entries in 1,836 frames, not worth the room.

    Helps withSpeed (CPU)
    Estimated effectNo speed effect

By place

Every place, character and object in the game, with every trick and item from every kit that touches it, and what is checked off. A trick can sit under several entries; tricks that help the whole game (the runtime, the JIT, the memory pool, the runtime’s boot) sit once under Everywhere. Tap an entry to open it, and a kit to read the full explanation on its card.

Known issues still open 3

  • Open · off by defaultKit 11's exact GPU rows were slower on the Vitadetails
  • Open · decision pendingThe two game-AOT apps have 1 MB less free memorydetails
